TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: China grapples with a slowly deflating artificial intelligence bubble , officials in Beijing keep their eyes on the U.S. midterms , and China and Pakistan launch an “information corridor” amid anxiety around their Belt and Road Initiative projects. U.S technology stocks are suffering a downturn amid worries about artificial intelligence overvaluation, but China’s AI market bubble has been slowly deflating for months. The country’s STAR 50 index of science and technology companies is down by more than 40 percent since July , leaving it nearly 200 points below where it was at the start of the year. The overall Shanghai stock exchange has also drifted down this year, but tech companies saw a significant rise until the summer, followed by a larger decline. It’s hard to pinpoint a clear reason for this drop. My own feeling is that the highest-quality U.S. AI models widened the distance with their Chinese counterparts this year, but that isn’t widely reflected in standard AI performance indexes , where the gap has largely stayed constant, falling somewhere between six to eight months for Chinese models to catch up with their U.S. equivalents. The increasingly strenuous compute demands of high-end AI are hard on Chinese firms, which possess a fraction of the computing power of their U.S. counterparts. That caused some cutbacks in providing services through the AI agents that are uber-popular in the country, but that was already the case when Chinese AI stocks were still rising in the first part of the year. My sense is that the stock decline isn’t specific to AI, but reflects a wider downturn in market confidence among the small Chinese investors who make up the majority of the market. That fits with the general doldrums of China’s post-COVID economy; Beijing is struggling to get the wind back in its sails, but the public isn’t willing to play along. As usual, state-backed investment firms are injecting capital back into the markets under official instructions; this is still unlikely to staunch the bleeding .   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/03/china-ai-stock-market-downturn-bubble-economics/

In the 48 hours that followed the Houthi attacks on Riyadh’s main international airport, China’s major media outlets largely focused on an Iranian offer to help mediate between the two warring parties. China’s enthusiastic coverage of Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi’s proposal comes on the back of an earlier report in September that China was privately pushing Iran to help mediate between Saudi Arabia and the Houthis. But it also speaks to why Beijing has little interest in pressuring Iran to end the war on Washington’s terms. China supports the Middle East taking greater responsibility for its own security, and its desire to see a reduced U.S. role in the region has long been a shared goal in the Sino-Iranian relationship. For instance, in one 1981 study , Chinese scholars recommended that Beijing actively strengthen Iran’s position in the Gulf to hobble U.S. access to the region’s oil while seeking a carve-out for Chinese oil interests. And throughout the 1980-88 Iran-Iraq War, senior Chinese officials regularly called for the withdrawal of U.S. forces from the Gulf and subsequently protested the U.S. intervention on Kuwait’s behalf when Iraq invaded the country in 1990. More recently, China has toned down its calls for U.S. withdrawals from the region, but it still advocates for countries and regions to take responsibility for their own security interests in the multipolar world that China calls for. He gives a taxi driver his Moscow address, “Third Builder’s Street,” and arrives at an identical block of flats, where his key opens the door. The world that the movie describes was once much larger than Russia itself. You could arrive in Kyiv, Tbilisi, or Riga and speak Russian; quote the same Soviet films; laugh at the same jokes; and assume that underneath all the flags, languages, and newly drawn borders there was still some common social and cultural space holding the place together. * The Quiet Revolution in U.S.-Cypriot Relations
  Foreign Policy | 03 Nov 16:36 UTC
  As ties with Turkey frayed over the past decade, Washington has drawn closer to Nicosia.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Since the 1974 Turkish invasion of Cyprus, Washington has almost exclusively seen the island as a problem in need of solution. But that dynamic has now quietly changed. Over the past decade, the Republic of Cyprus (ROC) has positioned itself as a critical security partner for an increasingly receptive United States. The bilateral relationship has gradually expanded to include defense, trade, basing, military training and exercises, crisis-response cooperation, and regular strategic dialogue. Given Cyprus’s strategic location, it is perhaps surprising that the initial impetus for this growing security relationship came from Cyprus, not Washington. In short, despite its ongoing division , Cyprus is emerging as a security producer for the United States and the West. For Nicosia, this relationship serves as an effective deterrent against any future Turkish threat, which remains the ROC’s primary security preoccupation. Beyond that, deepening ties with the United States help boost Cyprus’s international status and provide cover for its growing arms relationships and other security ties with other states, such as France and Israel. Turkey has watched these developments warily, though so far without a forceful response. Genuine peace may not have been their real objective, however, as Kushner and Witkoff are both ardent Israel supporters who were even less likely to put serious pressure on Israel than former U.S. President Joe Biden (which is saying something). In this view, the Gaza agreement was a Potemkin peace deal whose main goal was to free Israeli hostages held by Hamas and get the conflict off the front pages while tying Israel’s hands as little as possible. If so, then one might concede that they accomplished their objective. But if the goal was peace, they failed. * Can Washington Afford to Deter Chinese Cyber Activity?
  Foreign Policy | 03 Nov 12:00 UTC
  Costly signaling to Beijing could hurt the U.S. economy.
  FULL TEXT: In August, the U.S. Justice Department and the FBI dismantled the online infrastructure behind two hacking platforms operated by a Chinese state-sponsored group. It had been targeting sensitive U.S. government networks since at least 2018. In October, the agencies seized hacking tools used by another such group whose operations the U.S. government had previously disrupted in 2024. Washington can deny Beijing’s hackers access to harmful capabilities. But the longevity and resurgence of the hackers’ campaigns raise a hard truth: Disruption alone cannot reverse the strategic advantages generated by years of Chinese cyber activity. In August, the U.S. Justice Department and the FBI dismantled the online infrastructure behind two hacking platforms operated by a Chinese state-sponsored group. It had been targeting sensitive U.S. government networks since at least 2018. In October, the agencies seized hacking tools used by another such group whose operations the U.S. government had previously disrupted in 2024. Washington can deny Beijing’s hackers access to harmful capabilities. But the longevity and resurgence of the hackers’ campaigns raise a hard truth: Disruption alone cannot reverse the strategic advantages generated by years of Chinese cyber activity. The conventional U.S. approach to cyber deterrence has treated Chinese operations as individual security incidents. But to deter China from accruing advantages through cyber activity, the United States must change the incentives of a vast hacking ecosystem built around the Chinese state in order to counteract its impacts on the United States’ military, technology, and trade. A shift is now underway in Washington. In March, the U.S. government’s 2026 Cyber Strategy promised sustained and aggressive action through public-private partnerships, which would combine offensive and defensive capabilities with economic and legal countermeasures. Recent reports from the Council on Foreign Relations (CFR) and Britain’s Royal United Services Institute similarly advocate for coordinating cyber operations with other instruments of state power. Such proposals appear to recognize that disrupting individual operations has not changed China’s broader cyber behavior. But while combining instruments may help disrupt individual operations and parts of the ecosystem, they may still fail to credibly deter the broader pattern of activity that generates cumulative advantage. Therein lies the more troubling possibility: that the Unite
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/03/china-cyberattacks-united-states-deterrence-hackers-competition/

Last week, a high-ranking set of Saudi, Pakistani, and Turkish officials came to Tehran, where they met an Iranian delegation led by Mohsen Rezaei. Rezaei, former commander of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), heads Iran’s Supreme National Security Council (SNSC). The SNSC is the country’s top decision-making body and brings together leaders from the IRGC and conventional military, along with representatives of the supreme leader and elected officials. In this meeting, Rezaei was joined by President Masoud Pezeshkian and Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi. That these officials—representing Iran’s top security body, its president, and its foreign minister—all participated in the meeting suggests a degree of consensus across Iran’s political spectrum. In the past, many Iranian diplomatic efforts had been the sole prerogative of the foreign ministry, without buy-in from military and security institutions, which made their implementation difficult. This meeting, on the other hand, demonstrates that, across different centers of power, Iran’s political and military establishment is serious about diplomacy. * State of the climate: 2026 likely to tie with 2024 as the warmest year on record
  Carbon Brief | 03 Nov 13:48 UTC
  With three-quarters of the year now past, it is virtually certain that 2026 will be the warmest or second-warmest year… The post State of the climate: 2026 likely to tie with 2024 as the warmest year on record appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: With three-quarters of the year now past, it is virtually certain that 2026 will be the warmest or second-warmest year on record. A rapidly strengthening El Niño , alongside human-caused global warming, made this year’s August the warmest on record and September the second-warmest. El Niño is the warm phase of a recurring climate pattern in the tropical Pacific that releases heat from the ocean into the atmosphere and temporarily boosts global temperatures. Carbon Brief’s analysis gives 2026 a 67% chance of surpassing 2024 as the warmest year on record across an average of six global temperature datasets. In January, it is likely the groups that report global surface temperatures will be divided on whether 2026 officially beat 2024’s record . Currently, the odds for a record year range from as high as 97% in Berkeley Earth’s dataset to less than 20% in European Copernicus/ECMWF’s ERA5 reanalysis product. With El Niño expected to intensify into the winter, Carbon Brief projects that 2027 will be 1.7C above pre-industrial levels, with a 96% chance that it is the warmest year on record. In this latest “state of the climate” quarterly update, Carbon Brief finds: Carbon Brief analyses records from six different groups that report global surface temperatures: There are a number of additional groups that feature in Carbon Brief’s annual “ state of the climate ” update, but are not included here due to a lack of regular monthly updates. The first nine months of 2026 were the second warmest on record in all six datasets, behind only 2024. The figure below shows annual temperatures in each dataset since 1970, along with the 2026 average to date. The year got off to a comparatively slow start. Each month from January to June ran cooler than the same month in 2024, as the lingering effects of weak La Niña conditions gave way to a developing El Niño. La Niña and El Nino are the cool and warm phases of the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO), a recurring climate pattern in the tropical Pacific that reshapes weather patterns around the world. That changed in the third quarter of the year. July 2026 was the second-warmest July in the six-dataset average and a narrow record in the NASA GISTEMP and NOAA datasets. (NOAA’s own assessment described it as tied with July 2024.) August then set a new record for the month by around 0.12C in the six-dataset average. It was the warmest August on record in every one of the six datasets, by margins ranging from 0.07C (HadCRUT5 and NOA
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/state-of-the-climate-2026-likely-to-tie-with-2024-as-the-warmest-year-on-record

* The Year Math Changed Forever
  Longreads | 03 Nov 17:38 UTC
  "A.I. is solving problems that stumped the field for decades. Mathematicians look to Terence Tao for guidance on their altered world."
  FULL TEXT: There is an explanatory elegance to Rivka Galchen’s writing for The New Yorker that I’ve admired for a long time . Here, it finds a superb partner in the mind of Terence Tao, a renowned mathematician. As artificial intelligence casually spits out answers to notably evasive problems, Tao and his colleagues wrestle with the existential implications for their field. Galchen’s profile of Tao is also a demonstration of mathematics as human lineage and artistic practice, a chain of problems that has long encouraged expansive thinking. That chain now appears strained to the point of breaking—a potential “decoupling,” Galchen writes, of “problem-solving and insight.” Don’t miss the superb ending on this one. “Let me be clear. There was no fun in this for me,” he said. “The fun was had before I gave it to this computer program. But talking to the A.I. about it—this is not fun.” He recalled, as a kid, falling in love with logic puzzles. What he loved was being in the dark, trying to find his way forward. “The fun part was not understanding, and struggling to understand,” he said. “Having all the solutions written down is the least fun part.” “Ultrafinitism, a philosophy that rejects the infinite, has long been dismissed as mathematical heresy. But it is also producing new insights in math and beyond.” “A new method for quantifying grandeur is reshuffling the pecking order of the planet’s most impressive peaks. Turns out Everest has steep competition.” “After finding the homeschooling life confining, the teen petitioned her way into a graduate class at Berkeley, where she ended up disproving a 40-year-old conjecture.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/03/terence-tao-navier-stokes-ai-math-millenium-prize/

In the last decade or so, the state’s name has become internet slang for what Merriam-Webster defines as “awkward, cringeworthy, or otherwise undesirable.” But long before it was a meme, Ohio held an unflattering place in the American imagination. To much of the country, the Buckeye State, like its miles of flatlands, is an archetype of the middle ground, a kind of baseline for the ordinary. The contemporary American folk singer John Gorka described his home state of New Jersey, the Big Apple’s less glamorous neighbor, as “like Ohio, but even more so.” Here, along the Midwest’s fields and byways, the best summers taste of frozen custard and Lake Erie perch, of sweet corn that is “knee-high by the Fourth of July.” Now, the warm days are waning, and a popular corn maze cuts through the bare stalks in the nearby Cuyahoga Valley. Soon to be behind us, too, is this year’s midterm election frenzy, during which Democrats and Republicans have scrambled to win over the country’s mythical median voter. And where better to stage that fight than in Ohio? Every couple of years, it seems, the state’s reputation as a kind of blank canvas lends it outsize political heft: Pundits and politicians throw various shades of red and blue at its citizenry, who, as anyone who’s lived here will tell you, neither seek nor particularly care for the attention. Ohio’s on-again, off-again popularity with the rest of the country makes sense, I suppose. Once a reliably “purple” place — just as likely to be among the electoral map’s red Republican states as its blue Democratic ones — it has seemed, over the last three presidential election cycles, to have swung decidedly right. But despite Donald Trump and his allies winning handily each time they have run here, his picks this year seem to be floundering. There’s the race for governor, in which an 11th-hour ad from billionaire Republican candidate Vivek Ramaswamy earned the Trump acolyte widespread, bipartisan rebuke. In it, his opponent’s estranged mother resurfaces to call her daughter, whose accusations of childhood abuse led to her stepfather’s indictment, a liar. That Amy Acton’s former boss, sitting Republican Gov. The use of the word “dictator” went down badly in a country where Mao’s legacy and character are still not questioned, even half a century after his death. Any satire involving the former leader is taboo. * Believing and thinking
  Aeon | 03 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The separation of church and state has an unspoken psychological basis: we choose what we believe, but not what we think - by Neil Van Leeuwen & Jacob Eisler Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Fryeburg, Maine, US. Photo by Andrew Lichtenstein/Corbis/Getty Images The idea of separating church and state – now entrenched in many modern liberal democracies – is in some ways strange. After all, governments must keep themselves informed and inform their citizens. That’s why they turn to the science of meteorology to predict weather patterns, to economics to understand patterns of growth and decline, and to the medical sciences to improve public health. So why wouldn’t governments also keep close alliances with religious institutions to know what’s going on with God? Separating church and state appears strange from other perspectives as well. In the historical sweep of large-scale human societies, it’s a relative newcomer, often associated with the United States’ founding father Thomas Jefferson. In 1802, Jefferson asserted the principle of separation in a letter to the Danbury Baptist Association in Connecticut: We’re fans of Jefferson’s principle, one of us (Neil Van Leeuwen) being a philosophy professor who grew up in the Christian Reformed Church, and the other (Jacob Eisler) being a law professor who regularly attends synagogue. But Jefferson’s letter makes putting the principle into practice sound simpler than it is – as though governments could be entirely hands-off when it comes to people’s beliefs, or ‘opinions’. That, however, can’t be right: engaging with and shaping political, social and other kinds of beliefs is actually a core function of government. Public education guides what people believe about everything from mathematics to history to astronomy. Governments also shape beliefs for policy reasons. If fire department officials learn that people believe a building is safe when it isn’t, they will try to change what people believe about the building’s safety. Belief is also a category of legal interest. If a psychotherapist, for example, believes a patient is a danger to himself or others, the therapist is obligated by law to report that belief. All this means that Jefferson’s ‘wall of separation’ can’t sensibly prohibit governments from intervening on beliefs of any type whatsoever. Rather, Jefferson – along with advocates of the principle who came after him – must have presupposed that there is something distinctive about religious beliefs that makes it reasonable to give them a different legal status. But what is this distinctive thing? Is it that religious beliefs concern God? No, because Buddhism is a religion with its own beliefs, 
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-psychology-of-separating-church-and-state

The man who had congratulated me—referring to me as his “friend” with such sincerity—was Robin Garbutt, and he’d called from Wealstun Prison, where he was, and still is, serving a life sentence for the murder of his wife, Diana. In 2021, I was researching a possible sequel to my first book, a biography of Ian Fleming, the creator of James Bond. While at the National Archives in West London, partly through a filing error and partly through my own boredom, I found myself looking at recent criminal case files rather than the wartime naval intelligence records I had come for. One caught my eye immediately: Diana Garbutt, a woman murdered in the flat above the post office she ran with her husband Robin in the North Yorkshire village of Melsonby. The only available document for her murder was the judge’s summing up, and it was damning. Robin had called the emergency services on the morning of March 23, 2010, saying their post office had been robbed at gunpoint. After the perpetrators left, he claimed he had run upstairs to find Diana dead. But no one in the village had seen any evidence of the robbery, even though it was a busy morning. * The self-making world
  Aeon | 02 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Long mocked and misunderstood, Lamarck’s radical idea that organisms shape themselves and their world is making a comeback - by Jessica Riskin Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A pod of humpback whales making its way through Alaska’s Inside Passage. Courtesy of Far North Footage/Getty Images The French naturalist Jean-Baptiste Lamarck was 65 when his great treatise on the transformation of life was published. With Philosophie zoologique (1809), translated a century later as Zoological Philosophy , Lamarck intended to establish an entirely new science, one built on the idea that all living beings are constantly transforming themselves, from the tiniest microbes to the most complex mammals, gaining new forms and new abilities. It wasn’t the bodily structures of organisms that determined their ways of life, Lamarck said, but just the opposite: their ways of life in response to their circumstances gave rise to their changing bodily forms. ‘Can there be any consideration in natural history more important,’ he demanded, ‘or deserving of greater attention, than the one I have just set forth?’ (My translation.) But if you’ve heard anything at all about Lamarck, it’s probably how wrong he was about one animal in particular, the giraffe, and how this giraffe fiasco epitomises his wrongness about everything . Lamarck suggested that, by stretching to reach leaves in high tree branches, giraffes slightly elongated their necks, creating tiny changes that were then handed down to their offspring, accumulating over many generations to produce the long necks we see today. Around the turn of the 20th century, Lamarck’s giraffe idea became a laughing stock when biologists roundly rejected the notion that bodily changes undergone in the course of an individual lifetime could be inherited in the next generation. Illustration from Histoire naturelle, générale et particulière (1749-1804) by Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon. Courtesy the Linda Hall Library It wasn’t only his ideas about long-necked mammals that came under attack. Lamarck’s interest in giraffes was just a passing mention in the midst of his voluminous writings, which set out a revolutionary, field-founding theory: organisms, he claimed, transform themselves and, in doing so, they remake the world. That idea has drawn scorn for more than two centuries. Though Lamarck is often overlooked, his science was foundational to modern biology and informed the theory of evolution that Charles Darwin later developed. Darwin followed Lamarck in believing in the agency of living beings to transform themselves and their environment. But most people don’t know about Lamarck’s influence on Darwin b
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-self-making-world-of-jean-baptiste-lamarck

  FULL TEXT: Until early September, my Facebook feed looked much as it always had. Then, on Sept. 3, Houthi fighters launched a major military offensive against government-held areas of western Yemen, seizing six districts within a week. A month later, the internationally recognized Yemeni government responded with Operation Dawn of Yemen, aimed not only at recapturing lost territory but also at driving the Houthis out of other areas under their control. As fighting intensified across the front lines, an unusual number of mourning posts began appearing in my Facebook feed. Facebook remains Yemen’s public square, a place where people mourn their dead, celebrate milestones, document their lives and argue fiercely over the country’s politics. By early October, as the Houthis advanced deeper into government-held areas of Taiz province and approached the eponymous city itself, my feed had turned into an endless stream of obituaries. Parents grieved for their sons. Wives mourned their husbands. Friends wrote heartbreaking tributes to men they had known since childhood. As I began reading and collecting these posts, Facebook’s algorithm appeared to take notice of my interest. Each time I opened the platform, more photographs of the dead appeared, many shared by people who were not even on my friends list. Scrolling through Facebook began to feel like walking past a wall covered with photographs of the dead. “Hisham, the hero, my brother and beloved martyr, please come back, you troublemaker. My heart aches from your absence,” wrote Shehab al-Shehabi, a journalist, on Facebook on Oct. 13, sharing a photograph of Hisham, who looked barely out of childhood, dressed in a shirt, with a tan tactical vest. Unlike in Houthi-controlled areas, where information about fighters’ deaths appears to be tightly controlled and casualties are often announced through official funerals, families and friends in government-controlled areas openly share photographs, names and personal stories of those killed on the battlefield, making public expressions of grief more visible and spontaneous. * In praise of the dilettante
  Aeon | 30 Oct 10:00 UTC
  A wayfaring cosmopolitan who never gets stuck, the dilettante is the right anti-hero for our hyper-specialised age - by Jay Miller Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jean Cocteau, poet, artist, filmmaker, photographed in 1948 by Philippe Halsman. Courtesy Magnum Photos Why, of all people, defend the dilettante ? An amateur at best, a flighty rascal at worst: why take an interest in someone whose own interests are never more than a passing hobby? These are serious times – why take seriously someone for whom nothing is serious? Merriam-Webster defines the dilettante as ‘a person having a superficial interest in an art or a branch of knowledge’. Encyclopedia.com calls the dilettante someone who cultivates an interest ‘without real commitment or knowledge’. Cambridge settles for someone whose understanding ‘is not very deep or serious’. Collins gives us someone who ‘seems interested in a subject … but does not really know very much about it’. None of it is flattering. Clearly, the dilettante is no aspirational character. No university markets itself as training the next generation of dilettantes; no magazine runs a ‘dilettante of the year’ issue (except, perhaps, for the excellent Idler publication). He – for the dilettante is, historically and in popular imagination, so often a ‘he’ – is in essence an amateur, a role defined by wide interests and narrow expertise. Such is the reputation of the dilettante, assigned and maintained by a culture that treats a single-minded devotion to one pursuit as the only route to a meaningful life. Pick something and stick with it, we are told. It’s not so much an explicit campaign against dilettantism I’m challenging, but rather the diffuse yet relentless pressure to narrow one’s interests, to specialise, to gain expertise, to forgo breadth for the sake of depth. We hear it in the barrage of imperatives to follow your dreams or find your passion uttered at graduation speeches, in self-help guides, and similarly corporate-sounding pep talks. Under this scheme, the specialist is the hero and the dilettante is but a cautionary tale: the person who never committed, never finished, never got good enough at any one thing. But what if the scheme has it backwards? What if the dilettante’s perpetual movement from one thing to another is not a failure of nerve but a different and defensible way of being, one whose exploratory zeal turns out to be just what a hyper-specialised, over-optimised and all-too- serious world needs most? Then the dilettante would turn out to be not a capricious buffoon but a kind of modern anti-hero. That, I argue, is a dilettantism worth defending. F irst, let’s consider

The scenes sum up the state of independent journalism in Egypt, amid a fraught political climate and mixed signals that swing between a security clampdown and the prospect of a loosening of restrictions. In late September, the original owners marched on both sites and stopped the machinery. The government responded that the expropriations were legal and all requisite compensation had been paid. Even for people from the area like me, the protests and the public debate since the downfall of the Assad regime have revealed the scale of the issue in ways we had not previously understood. In the towns along the Damascus-Beirut highway alone, residents count tens of thousands of acres taken for state housing under two decrees signed by Hafez al-Assad in the mid-1970s, with thousands more in Dummar. The Assad regime used the expropriation law to build military bases, security branches and loyalist housing around the Syrian capital. Much of the land the regime took is still in the state’s hands, either fenced off or built on. The new authorities have inherited that issue, but, to the farmers of my father’s generation, they have so far shown no intention of correcting a basic injustice. The distinction that will ultimately matter is … whether AI can serve its purpose and be controlled locally. But in Africa’s capitals, the distinction that will ultimately matter is not whose product was used to build a system, or who dominates whom, but rather whether AI can serve its purpose and be controlled locally. African countries are already building AI for digital transformation, as outlined in the African Union’s continental AI strategy and country-specific AI strategies. * How Rome’s Urban Shepherds Adapt and Survive
  New Lines | 28 Oct 12:39 UTC
  For millennia, shepherds have herded their sheep through Rome to graze, adapting to modernity and the urban landscape. But the coexistence of animals and humans is becoming more fraught. The post How Rome’s Urban Shepherds Adapt and Survive appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: As the sun sets over the Castelli Romani in the distance, Antonio herds his sheep back toward the fold. We are on the hills of Monte Cucco, on Rome’s southern outskirts. Here, hemmed in by the concrete buildings of the working-class district of Trullo, Antonio’s father once fought as a partisan in the Italian resistance. On the horizon looms the “Square Colosseum,” Mussolini’s monument to fascist grandeur, a reminder of Italy’s architectural integration of this part of its past rather than its total erasure. The sheep, it seems, pay it no mind: They have grazed and resisted change in this land for centuries, in the least expected places. In the Italian capital, sheep have always been part of the metropolitan landscape. It’s estimated there are roughly 50,000 of them spread all over the city. In Monte Cucco, Antonio’s 200 or so animals can be seen grazing. Later, after these interviews but before the time of writing, the flock will be removed to make way for a so-called requalification project that doesn’t envision their presence: a modern park. Most of the sheep will be sent to slaughterhouses. Others will be sold. And as a result, because there are no sheep to tame it, the grass on the hill of Monte Cucco will grow tall, reaching more than 6 feet high, and fires will break out. Rome (and, by extension, perhaps other cities in Italy) has to contend with a fundamental question: What to do with such ancient traditions? Some residents can picture coexistence with the animals, while others fear that “children will get too close to the droppings,” as one resident says. Chiara Visentin, scientific director of the Emilio Sereni Library-Archive at the Alcide Cervi Institute, explains that the legacy of the Enlightenment period can be seen today in people’s repulsion at living alongside the animals, which was once considered normal but is now automatically associated with unpleasant smells, disease and inconvenience. And although the tradition is challenged in Rome, it remains visible.   FULL TEXT: More than a few readers may squirm as they nibble their way through Sophie Haigney’s latest, about eaters who are rejecting “the usual sources of authority” and tinkering with optimization through unconventional diets—upping their intake of raw buffalo brain, for instance, or swearing off vegetables. But Haigney’s not in it for the disgust or the judgment. Instead, she moves among “dark nutrition” practitioners with a deep curiosity for how they try to manage uncertainty through appetite. It was like any other absorbing pastime: You could tunnel deep into nutrition, endlessly studying and adjusting your diet, treating your body as an ongoing experiment. When traditional researchers do experiments, they use n to represent the sample size, like the number of individuals tested. Dark-nutrition circles were constantly talking about the “ n of one” — the idea that the only way to learn what is good for you is by ignoring broad samples and experimenting on your unique self. “The search for an answer took us to a mustard dynasty in France, a Hamilton mustard mill and Palliser’s Triangle.” “The California giant has helped turn a local, seasonal treat into a worldwide refrigerator staple and marketing juggernaut.” “Turf wars. * The Amarna letters
  Aeon | 28 Oct 10:01 UTC
  Petty disputes, pleas for help and requests for gold – explore letters sent to Egyptian pharaohs more than 3,000 years ago - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: First discovered in Egypt in the late 19th century, the collection of clay tablets known as the Amarna letters is one of our richest windows into the ancient world. The letters are extraordinary not only for their contents – diplomatic communications exchanged between Egyptian pharaohs and other nearby rulers in the 14th century BCE – but also for their script: they were written not in Egyptian hieroglyphs but largely in cuneiform, a writing system most often associated with ancient Mesopotamia. In this video from the British Museum, the always entertaining Assyriologist Irving Finkel details how the letters came to be and how they were discovered, and delves into the fascinating correspondence itself, which includes petty disputes over perceived slights, leaders lobbying for military support and more than a few requests for gold.
